someone I used to be.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: When I try to balance the senior-center budget or read through a program schedule, the numbers and dates blur together and I have to check the same simple thing over and over because my mind will not hold onto it.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: A crosswalk signal, the growl of a truck, or even a walking-tour map can suddenly put the whole scene back in my head so sharply that I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I go out of my way to avoid that intersection and I have stopped signing up for most tours or safety meetings because I cannot bear the conversations that circle back to what happened there.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep telling myself that if I had picked a different route, or pushed harder about that crossing, she might still be alive, and then I get furious all over again at the driver and the town for letting it happen.\n- key: S074\n  name: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed, and it has not eased the way everyone seemed to think it would.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: Even when I sit with people I have known for years at the senior center, I feel separated from them, as if they are chatting on one side of a window and I am on the other.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I snap at neighbors over small things like a car idling too long or someone blocking the sidewalk, and afterward I am embarrassed because the sharpness in my voice sounds nothing like me.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: Some nights I get in the car and drive around checking corners, signs, and crosswalks even when I know I am tired and should not be on the road.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Whenever I am near traffic, I watch every truck, curb, and pedestrian like it is my responsibility to catch danger before it happens again.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have had to step back from organizing senior-center programs and managing volunteer budgets because I cannot keep up reliably, and I have pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to structure my week.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour about two years and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S074\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Was your friend's death something you witnessed or experienced directly?\n\nPatient: No, I... I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there. I just got the call about it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I... I go out of my way to avoid that intersection now. And I've stopped signing up for most tours or the community safety meetings. I just can't bear the conversations, because they always seem to circle back to what happened there, and I don't want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience unwanted memories or thoughts about your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It suddenly puts the whole scene right back in my head. It's so sharp that I can actually see my friend stepping off the curb, and it just flashes into my mind before I can even try to stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your guilt, fear, or anger interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home since your friend's death?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I mean... I don't feel like I'm watching myself from the outside. When those memories happen, I'm very much in my own body. It just feels like I'm right back on that street again, experiencing it. But no, I haven't noticed any feeling of being outside of myself or watching from afar.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I used to do quite a bit... I'd spend time planning my garden beds, I'd quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings. I even used to argue happily at my book club. But now... I just look at those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself enjoying or looking forward to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I can't think of anything right now that I actually look forward to. I just sort of get through the days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, beyond just not looking forward to them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before... I really don't have any interest in those things anymore. I used to love planning out my garden beds for the spring, or going to quilt with the ladies. I enjoyed going out to the theater, and I even liked going to my book club and getting into friendly arguments about whatever we were reading. But now, when I see those things written on my calendar, I just feel empty. It really feels like all those hobbies belong to a completely different person. I just don't care about them at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in social interactions as well, not just hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, it affects that too. Even when I make myself go down to the senior center and sit with people I've known for years... I just feel completely separated from them. It's hard to explain, but it's almost like... like they are all chatting on one side of a window, and I am just sitting on the other side. I'm physically there, but I'm not really part of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your participation in activities you used to find meaningful or enjoyable since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I used to be very involved... I actually used to organize some of the programs at the senior center and manage the volunteer budgets. But I've had to step back from all of that. I just can't keep up with it reliably anymore. And like I said, I've pulled away from my clubs and the other activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't participate much at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, to the point where it's significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I've pretty much covered that. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Was your friend's death something you witnessed or experienced directly?\n\nPatient: No, I... I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there. I just got the call about it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I... I go out of my way to avoid that intersection now. And I've stopped signing up for most tours or the community safety meetings. I just can't bear the conversations, because they always seem to circle back to what happened there, and I don't want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience unwanted memories or thoughts about your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It doesn't take much to bring it on, honestly. Just... the sound of a crosswalk signal, or hearing the loud growl of a passing truck. Even just seeing a walking-tour map can do it. It suddenly puts the whole scene right back in my head. It's so sharp that I can actually see my friend stepping off the curb, and it just flashes into my mind before I can even try to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Has your guilt, fear, or anger interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I've actually had to step back from organizing the programs at the senior center, and I gave up managing the volunteer budgets. I just... I can't keep up with it reliably anymore. My concentration isn't there. And I've pretty much pulled away from all the clubs and activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't have the energy or the focus for it right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on ever since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was... about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from afar when you're reminded of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that. I mean... I don't feel like I'm watching myself from the outside. When those memories happen, I'm very much in my own body. It just feels like I'm right back on that street again, experiencing it. But no, I haven't noticed any feeling of being outside of myself or watching from afar.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I used to do quite a bit... I'd spend time planning my garden beds, I'd quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings. I even used to argue happily at my book club. But now... I just look at those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself enjoying or looking forward to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I can't think of anything right now that I actually look forward to. I just sort of get through the days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, beyond just not looking forward to them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before... I really don't have any interest in those things anymore. I used to love planning out my garden beds for the spring, or going to quilt with the ladies. I enjoyed going out to the theater, and I even liked going to my book club and getting into friendly arguments about whatever we were reading. But now, when I see those things written on my calendar, I just feel empty. It really feels like all those hobbies belong to a completely different person. I just don't care about them at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in social interactions as well, not just hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, it affects that too. Even when I make myself go down to the senior center and sit with people I've known for years... I just feel completely separated from them. It's hard to explain, but it's almost like... like they are all chatting on one side of a window, and I am just sitting on the other side. I'm physically there, but I'm not really part of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your participation in activities you used to find meaningful or enjoyable since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I used to be very involved... I actually used to organize some of the programs at the senior center and manage the volunteer budgets. But I've had to step back from all of that. I just can't keep up with it reliably anymore. And like I said, I've pulled away from my clubs and the other activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't participate much at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, to the point where it's significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I've pretty much covered that. Like I said, I've stopped doing my hobbies, I stepped down from my volunteer work at the center, and I don't really socialize anymore. So yes, I'd say it's definitely impacted my daily life. There isn't much left that I actually do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel a little more like participating in things, or has it been a consistent lack of interest?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's... it's been pretty consistent, honestly. Every day just feels kind of the same. I haven't really had any days where I wake up and suddenly feel like getting back into things. It's just a steady lack of interest, no matter what day it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It's hard to explain, but it's almost like... like they are all chatting on one side of a window, and I am just sitting on the other side. I'm physically there, but I'm not really part of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your participation in activities you used to find meaningful or enjoyable since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I used to be very involved... I actually used to organize some of the programs at the senior center and manage the volunteer budgets. But I've had to step back from all of that. I just can't keep up with it reliably anymore. And like I said, I've pulled away from my clubs and the other activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't participate much at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, to the point where it's significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I've pretty much covered that. Like I said, I've stopped doing my hobbies, I stepped down from my volunteer work at the center, and I don't really socialize anymore. So yes, I'd say it's definitely impacted my daily life. There isn't much left that I actually do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel a little more like participating in things, or has it been a consistent lack of interest?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's... it's been pretty consistent, honestly. Every day just feels kind of the same. I haven't really had any days where I wake up and suddenly feel like getting back into things. It's just a steady lack of interest, no matter what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy to the point where it feels like a big part of you is missing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that's exactly how it feels. It really does feel like a big part of me is missing. I used to do so much... I'd plan out my garden beds, go quilting with the ladies, take theater outings, and I always loved happily arguing over things at my book club. But now, I just look at those things written on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be, not me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... since my friend was killed, I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It hasn't eased up at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would by now. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Was your friend's death something you witnessed or experienced directly?\n\nPatient: No, I... I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there. I just got the call about it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I... I go out of my way to avoid that intersection now. And I've stopped signing up for most tours or the community safety meetings. I just can't bear the conversations, because they always seem to circle back to what happened there, and I don't want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience unwanted memories or thoughts about your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It doesn't take much to bring it on, honestly. Just... the sound of a crosswalk signal, or hearing the loud growl of a passing truck. Even just seeing a walking-tour map can do it. It suddenly puts the whole scene right back in my head. It's so sharp that I can actually see my friend stepping off the curb, and it just flashes into my mind before I can even try to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Has your guilt, fear, or anger interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I've actually had to step back from organizing the programs at the senior center, and I gave up managing the volunteer budgets. I just... I can't keep up with it reliably anymore. My concentration isn't there. And I've pretty much pulled away from all the clubs and activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't have the energy or the focus for it right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on ever since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was... about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from afar when you're reminded of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that. I mean... I don't feel like I'm watching myself from the outside. When those memories happen, I'm very much in my own body. It just feels like I'm right back on that street again, experiencing it. But no, I haven't noticed any feeling of being outside of myself or watching from afar.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I used to do quite a bit... I'd spend time planning my garden beds, I'd quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings. I even used to argue happily at my book club. But now... I just look at those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself enjoying or looking forward to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I can't think of anything right now that I actually look forward to. I just sort of get through the days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, beyond just not looking forward to them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before... I really don't have any interest in those things anymore. I used to love planning out my garden beds for the spring, or going to quilt with the ladies. I enjoyed going out to the theater, and I even liked going to my book club and getting into friendly arguments about whatever we were reading. But now, when I see those things written on my calendar, I just feel empty. It really feels like all those hobbies belong to a completely different person. I just don't care about them at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in social interactions as well, not just hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, it affects that too. Even when I make myself go down to the senior center and sit with people I've known for years... I just feel completely separated from them. It's hard to explain, but it's almost like... like they are all chatting on one side of a window, and I am just sitting on the other side. I'm physically there, but I'm not really part of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your participation in activities you used to find meaningful or enjoyable since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I used to be very involved... I actually used to organize some of the programs at the senior center and manage the volunteer budgets. But I've had to step back from all of that. I just can't keep up with it reliably anymore. And like I said, I've pulled away from my clubs and the other activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't participate much at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, to the point where it's significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I've pretty much covered that. Like I said, I've stopped doing my hobbies, I stepped down from my volunteer work at the center, and I don't really socialize anymore. So yes, I'd say it's definitely impacted my daily life. There isn't much left that I actually do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel a little more like participating in things, or has it been a consistent lack of interest?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's... it's been pretty consistent, honestly. Every day just feels kind of the same. I haven't really had any days where I wake up and suddenly feel like getting back into things. It's just a steady lack of interest, no matter what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy to the point where it feels like a big part of you is missing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that's exactly how it feels. It really does feel like a big part of me is missing. I used to do so much... I'd plan out my garden beds, go quilting with the ladies, take theater outings, and I always loved happily arguing over things at my book club. But now, I just look at those things written on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be, not me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything that you still find pleasure in or that brings you joy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I was just saying about the gardening, the quilting group, and the book club... those were the things that gave me joy. Now I just look at those events on the calendar and feel pretty much nothing. Since all of those activities feel like they belong to someone I used to be, there really isn't anything left right now that I actually find pleasure in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... since my friend was killed, I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It hasn't eased up at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would by now. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Was your friend's death something you witnessed or experienced directly?\n\nPatient: No, I... I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there. I go out of my way to avoid that intersection now. And I've stopped signing up for most tours or the community safety meetings. It doesn't take much to bring it on, honestly. Just... the sound of a crosswalk signal, or hearing the loud growl of a passing truck. Even just seeing a walking-tour map can do it. It suddenly puts the whole scene right back in my head. It's so sharp that I can actually see my friend stepping off the curb, and it just flashes into my mind before I can even try to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Has your guilt, fear, or anger interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I've actually had to step back from organizing the programs at the senior center, and I gave up managing the volunteer budgets. I just... I can't keep up with it reliably anymore. I just don't have the energy or the focus for it right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on ever since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was... about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from afar when you're reminded of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that. I mean... I don't feel like I'm watching myself from the outside. When those memories happen, I'm very much in my own body. It just feels like I'm right back on that street again, experiencing it. But no, I haven't noticed any feeling of being outside of myself or watching from afar.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I do blame myself, yes. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route that day, or if I had pushed harder about how dangerous that crossing was... she might still be alive. And then, I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting it happen in the first place. But mostly, I just think about what I should have done differently.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... since my friend was killed, I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It hasn't eased up at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would by now. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Was your friend's death something you witnessed or experienced directly?\n\nPatient: No, I... I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there. I just got the call about it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I... I go out of my way to avoid that intersection now. And I've stopped signing up for most tours or the community safety meetings. I just can't bear the conversations, because they always seem to circle back to what happened there, and I don't want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience unwanted memories or thoughts about your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It doesn't take much to bring it on, honestly. Just... the sound of a crosswalk signal, or hearing the loud growl of a passing truck. Even just seeing a walking-tour map can do it. It suddenly puts the whole scene right back in my head. It's so sharp that I can actually see my friend stepping off the curb, and it just flashes into my mind before I can even try to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Has your guilt, fear, or anger interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I've actually had to step back from organizing the programs at the senior center, and I gave up managing the volunteer budgets. I just... I can't keep up with it reliably anymore. My concentration isn't there. And I've pretty much pulled away from all the clubs and activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't have the energy or the focus for it right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on ever since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was... about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from afar when you're reminded of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like that. I mean... I don't feel like I'm watching myself from the outside. When those memories happen, I'm very much in my own body. It just feels like I'm right back on that street again, experiencing it. But no, I haven't noticed any feeling of being outside of myself or watching from afar.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I used to do quite a bit... I'd spend time planning my garden beds, I'd quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings. I even used to argue happily at my book club. But now... I just look at those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself enjoying or looking forward to?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I can't think of anything right now that I actually look forward to. I just sort of get through the days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, beyond just not looking forward to them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before... I really don't have any interest in those things anymore. I used to love planning out my garden beds for the spring, or going to quilt with the ladies. I enjoyed going out to the theater, and I even liked going to my book club and getting into friendly arguments about whatever we were reading. But now, when I see those things written on my calendar, I just feel empty. It really feels like all those hobbies belong to a completely different person. I just don't care about them at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in social interactions as well, not just hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, it affects that too. Even when I make myself go down to the senior center and sit with people I've known for years... I just feel completely separated from them. It's hard to explain, but it's almost like... like they are all chatting on one side of a window, and I am just sitting on the other side. I'm physically there, but I'm not really part of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your participation in activities you used to find meaningful or enjoyable since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I used to be very involved... I actually used to organize some of the programs at the senior center and manage the volunteer budgets. But I've had to step back from all of that. I just can't keep up with it reliably anymore. And like I said, I've pulled away from my clubs and the other activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't participate much at all these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy, to the point where it's significantly impacted your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I've pretty much covered that. Like I said, I've stopped doing my hobbies, I stepped down from my volunteer work at the center, and I don't really socialize anymore. So yes, I'd say it's definitely impacted my daily life. There isn't much left that I actually do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel a little more like participating in things, or has it been a consistent lack of interest?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's... it's been pretty consistent, honestly. Every day just feels kind of the same. I haven't really had any days where I wake up and suddenly feel like getting back into things. It's just a steady lack of interest, no matter what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy to the point where it feels like a big part of you is missing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that's exactly how it feels. It really does feel like a big part of me is missing. I used to do so much... I'd plan out my garden beds, go quilting with the ladies, take theater outings, and I always loved happily arguing over things at my book club. But now, I just look at those things written on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It honestly feels like those activities belong to someone I used to be, not me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's anything that you still find pleasure in or that brings you joy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I mentioned, I used to look forward to so much... planning the garden, the quilting group, theater trips, the book club... but now they're just words on a calendar. I don't feel any excitement or anticipation for them at all anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you're a bad person or that you're to blame for your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I do blame myself, yes. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route that day, or if I had pushed harder about how dangerous that crossing was... she might still be alive. And then, I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting it happen in the first place. But mostly, I just think about what I should have done differently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
